MINISTRY OF EDUCATION AND TRAINING FPT UNIVERSITY
Detecting COVID Through Coughing Using Deep Learning Algorithms
by
TranMinhKha
A thesis submitted in conformity with the requirements for the degree of Master of Software Engineering
Supervisor:

2. Dr. Le The Anh.
Â© Copyright by Tran Minh Kha 2026
Detecting COVID Through Coughing Using Deep Learning Algorithms 
Tran Minh Kha
Degree Master of Software Engineering FPT University 2026
Abstract
This research investigates the development of an automated COVID-19 detection system through the analysis of cough audio signals using advanced deep learning architectures. We propose two primary approaches: (1) a 1-Dimensional (1D) raw waveform analysis utilizing the 
Wav2Vec 2.0
 framework, and (2) a 2-Dimensional (2D) spectrogram-based analysis using the 
Audio Spectrogram Transformer (AST)
. The core contribution of this study is the integration of 
Deep Neural Decision Forests (DNDF)
 as the classification layer, replacing traditional linear layers to enhance decision-making robustness. Empirical results on the COUGHVID dataset demonstrate that the 1D Wav2Vec 2.0-DNDF model outperforms other configurations, achieving a mean 
Area Under the Curve (AUC) of 0.748
. These findings suggest that self-supervised pre-trained models combined with neural-structured forests provide a promising direction for non-invasive respiratory disease screening
.
Keywordsâ€” 
COVID-19 detection, cough sound analysis, deep learning, Wav2Vec 2.0, Audio Spectrogram Transformer (AST), Deep Neural Decision Forest (DNDF), Explainable AI (XAI), LIME, 
Multi-source
 validation, respiratory disease screening.

Chapter 1 
INTRODUCTION
1.1. Definitions of Terms
Acoustic Biomarkers:
 Measurable indicators extracted from human respiratory sounds (cough, breath, speech) that reflect the physiological state of the respiratory tract and lungs.
Deep Neural Decision Forest (DNDF):
 A hybrid architecture that integrates the representation learning capabilities of Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) with the robust, non-linear classification of Decision Forests, enabling end-to-end trainable stochastic routing.
Domain-Shift (Dataset Shift):
 The discrepancy between the probability distributions of training (source) and testing (target) data, often leading to a significant drop in predictive performance when a model is deployed in a new environment.
Explainable AI (XAI):
 Frameworks and techniques (e.g., 
LIME - Local Interpretable Model-agnostic Explanations
) designed to make the internal "black-box" decision logic transparent by approximating the model locally with an interpretable one.
Mel-Spectrogram:
 A visual representation of the spectrum of frequencies of a signal as it varies with time, mapped onto the Mel scale to mimic human auditory perception.
Figure 
1
 Example of a Mel-spectrogram representation of a cough signal.
1.2. Research Context and Motivation
The global health crisis precipitated by the SARS-CoV-2 pandemic has fundamentally reshaped the paradigm of infectious disease monitoring. Although the world has transitioned into a post-pandemic era, the persistent evolution of viral variants and the threat of future respiratory pathogens underscore the necessity for scalable, digital diagnostic infrastructure. Traditional diagnostic protocols, notably the Reverse Transcription Polymerase Chain Reaction (RT-PCR), represent the clinical gold standard but are hindered by logistical complexities, including the need for specialized laboratory hardware and prolonged processing times.
In this context, audio-based screening emerges as a transformative solution. Physiological research indicates that COVID-19 causes significant biomechanical alterations in the respiratory system, including inflammation of the bronchial tubes and changes in lung elasticity. These alterations manifest as unique "acoustic signatures" in a patientâ€™s cough. Unlike healthy individuals, the cough of a COVID-19 patient often exhibits specific frequency distributions and temporal patterns. Leveraging the ubiquity of smartphones, an AI-driven cough analysis system could provide an instantaneous, zero-cost screening tool, effectively "triaging" individuals before they undergo expensive clinical testing.
Figure 
2
 Conceptual pipeline of an AI-based cough analysis system for COVID-19 screening.
It is crucial to clarify that the proposed AI framework is intended as a 
preliminary screening (triage) tool
 rather than a definitive clinical diagnostic system. By acting as a first-line 'filter,' the system aims to 
optimize medical resources
 by prioritizing high-risk individuals for confirmatory RT-PCR testing, especially in resource-constrained environments. This approach allows for instantaneous, zero-cost screening, effectively 'triaging' individuals before they undergo more expensive and time-consuming clinical protocols.
1.3. Problem Statement
Despite the proliferation of Deep Learning (DL) models for COVID-19 detection, two critical gaps prevent their transition from laboratory prototypes to clinical tools:
The Generalization Gap (Domain-Shift):
 Most state-of-the-art models are trained and validated on a single, clean dataset (e.g., 
Coswara
). However, when these models encounter "unseen" data from different geographical regions, different microphone qualities, or varying levels of ambient noise (e.g., COUGHVID or 
Virufy
), their accuracy often collapses. This is due to the model learning "dataset-specific" noise rather than universal clinical biomarkers.
Figure 
3
 Conceptual pipeline of an AI-based cough analysis system for COVID-19 screening.
The Interpretability Gap (Black-box Nature):
 Standard Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) operate as "black boxes," providing a probability score without clinical justification. For a diagnostic tool to be trusted by the medical community, it must demonstrate 
why
 a specific cough was labeled as positiveâ€”whether it was due to a specific wheezing frequency or a change in the coughâ€™s power spectrum.
Figure 
4
 Illustration of domain shift between training and unseen datasets.
Without addressing these two pillarsâ€”
Robustness
 and 
Explainability
â€”AI-based respiratory diagnosis remains a theoretical exercise rather than a practical medical utility.
1.4. Research Objectives
The primary objective of this thesis is to develop a highly generalized and interpretable deep learning framework for COVID-19 detection. Specific sub-objectives include:
Developing a hybrid CNN-DNDF architecture to replace deterministic 
Softmax
 layers with stochastic decision trees for better handling of data uncertainty.
Figure 
5
 Proposed hybrid CNN-DNDF architecture.
Implementing a multi-source data fusion strategy (
Coswara
, COUGHVID, 
Virufy
) to expose the model to diverse recording conditions.
Applying LIME techniques to localize the most influential acoustic features in the frequency domain, providing a visual "heat map" for clinical validation.
1.5. Research Questions
RQ1:
 To what extent does the integration of Deep Neural Decision Forests (DNDF) improve the model's ability to maintain high sensitivity across heterogeneous datasets compared to standard CNNs?
RQ2:
 How does the fusion of crowdsourced datasets with varying signal-to-noise ratios (SNR) affect the robustness of feature extraction?
RQ3:
 Can the 
LIME feature importance scores
 consistently identify acoustic features that align with the known physiological characteristics of COVID-19 coughs?
1.5.1. Research Hypotheses
<w:tab w:val="left" w:pos="840"/></w:tabs></w:pPr><w:r><w:rPr><w:b/><w:bCs/><w:color w:val="303030"/><w:shd w:val="clear" w:color="auto" w:fill="FFFFFF"/></w:rPr><w:t>H1:
 
The integration of Deep Neural Decision Forests (DNDF) as a classification layer will provide superior decision robustness and lower variance compared to traditional linear-
softmax
 layers.
<w:tab w:val="left" w:pos="840"/></w:tabs></w:pPr><w:r><w:rPr><w:b/><w:bCs/><w:color w:val="303030"/><w:shd w:val="clear" w:color="auto" w:fill="FFFFFF"/></w:rPr><w:t>H2:
 
Applying 
MixUp
 at the latent manifold level will preserve the acoustic phase integrity of 1D audio signals more effectively than raw input-level mixing.

 LIME-based interpretability will reveal high-activation zones in frequency bands (2 kHzâ€“6 kHz) that align with clinical acoustic markers of viral respiratory distress."
1.6. Research Contributions
Technical Contribution:
 The design of a CNN-DNDF hybrid that mitigates the "overfitting" common in small-scale medical audio datasets.
Methodological Contribution:
 A systematic evaluation of "
Multi-source
 Validation," where the model is trained on one dataset and tested on another to simulate real-world deployment.
Clinical Contribution:
 The introduction of an XAI-based verification layer that enhances the "trustworthiness" of the AI's diagnostic output for healthcare providers.
1.7. Scope and Limitations
This research focuses strictly on the analysis of 
forced cough sounds
 and does not include other symptoms like body temperature or heart rate. The datasets used are restricted to open-source, crowdsourced repositories (
Coswara
, COUGHVID, 
Virufy
). A key limitation is the inherent noise in crowdsourced data, which may include background speech or non-cough sounds, requiring rigorous pre-processing.
A further limitation pertains to label quality: the COUGHVID dataset employs a self-reporting paradigm where COVID-19 status is patient-declared rather than PCR-confirmed. This introduces label noise that may overstate model performance. Future work should evaluate on a filtered subset using samples with high 
expert_evaluation_score
 (â‰¥0.8) and ideally incorporate clinician-validated ground truth labels.
1.8. Thesis Structure
The remainder of this thesis is organized as follows:
Chapter 2: Related Work
 â€“ Provides a critical review of at least 50 existing studies on audio-based respiratory diagnosis and identifies current gaps in the literature.
Chapter 3: Theoretical Basis
 â€“ Discusses the fundamentals of signal processing (STFT, Mel-scale) and the mathematical foundations of CNNs and DNDFs.
Chapter 4: Methodology
 â€“ Details the proposed workflow, including data pre-processing, architecture design, and the XAI integration.
Chapter 5: Experiments and Results
 â€“ Presents the performance metrics (Accuracy, Sensitivity, AUC-ROC) and the 
Multi-source
 evaluation results.
Chapter 6: Conclusion and Future Work
 â€“ Summarizes the findings and suggests directions for real-time deployment and clinical integration.
Chapter 2 RELATED WORK
2.1. Overview of the Research Landscape
The rapid evolution of AI-driven COVID-19 detection from cough sounds can be viewed through three distinct technological waves. The first wave focused on establishing massive crowdsourced datasets such as 
Coswara
 and COUGHVID. The second wave saw the dominance of standard Deep Learning (DL) architectures (CNNs, RNNs) which achieved high accuracy on closed datasets. The current third wave, which this thesis occupies, focuses on solving the "Generalization Gap" through robust architectures like Deep Neural Decision Forests (DNDF) and providing clinical transparency via Explainable AI (XAI).
2.2. Foundational Datasets and Benchmarking
The feasibility of this field was established by the release of large-scale, open-source repositories. 
Sharma et al. (2020) [3]
 introduced the 
Coswara
 dataset, providing a diverse set of respiratory sounds from the Indian subcontinent. Shortly after, 
Orlandic
 et al. (2021) [2]
 launched 
COUGHVID
, a global crowdsourcing initiative reaching over 25,000 samples.
However, as highlighted in the systematic review by 
Askari 
Nasab
 et al. (2023) [10]
, these datasets introduced significant "label noiseâ€ where the quality of recording devices and environmental interference vary drastically. 
Mouawad et al. (2021) [4]
 conducted a robustness analysis showing that models trained on these datasets are highly susceptible to background noise, necessitating advanced pre-processing or more resilient classification heads.
A critical consideration in current research is 
Dataset Bias
. Beyond geographical biasâ€”such as the India-centric nature of 
Coswara
â€”researchers must account for 
linguistic and vocal-tract biases
. While coughing is a physiological reflex, acoustic signatures can be subtly influenced by regional linguistic traits, regional accents, and glottal dynamics associated with native languages. This creates a significant 
'Domain-Shift'
 that models must overcome to achieve true global generalization. To address these challenges, this study performs 
cross-dataset validation (
Coswara
, COUGHVID, and 
Virufy
)
 to evaluate the model's ability to generalize across diverse demographic and geographic profiles, moving beyond dataset-specific noise to identify universal clinical biomarkers
.
2.3. Evolution of Deep Learning Architectures
The transition from handcrafted features to automated feature extraction marked a significant leap in performance.
Standard CNNs:
 
Pahar
 et al. (2021) [5]
 demonstrated that CNNs applied to Mel-spectrograms could outperform traditional SVMs. Building on this, the 
Covid Cough Net (2023) [16]
 utilized a combination of 
InceptionFireNet
 and 
DeepConvNet
 to achieve an accuracy of over 95%.
Advanced Representations:
 To capture more complex signal dynamics, 
Phase Space Reconstruction (PSR)
 was introduced. A landmark study in 
2025 [12]
 utilized 
3D DCNNs
 on tensors derived from PSR, showing that 3D convolutions can capture temporal-spectral correlations that 2D CNNs miss.
Segmentation and Pre-processing:
 Recent work in 
2024 [17]
 proved that refining the input via specialized cough signal segmentation significantly reduces the false-positive rate compared to using raw, unclipped audio.
2.4. Addressing the Generalization Gap (Domain-Shift)
The most critical limitation in current literature is the failure of models to maintain performance across different datasets (Domain-Shift). 
Khalid et al. (2026) [8]
 provided a comprehensive cross-dataset evaluation, revealing that a model's AUC can drop by 20-30% when moved from 
Coswara
 to COUGHVID.
To mitigate this, 
Islam et al. (2025) [1]
 proposed the 
Deep Neural Decision Forest (DNDF)
. Unlike traditional CNNs that use a deterministic 
Softmax
 layer, DNDF integrates stochastic routing via decision trees. This allows the model to handle the "uncertainty" inherent in noisy audio signals more effectively. Our research extends this by not only using DNDF but also fusing three major datasets to broaden the model's exposure to diverse recording conditions.
2.5. Explainable AI (XAI) and Clinical Trust
For AI to be adopted in hospitals, doctors must understand the "why" behind a diagnosis.
Saliency Maps:
 Research in 
2022 [18]
 utilized Saliency Maps to highlight which parts of a spectrogram contributed most to a positive COVID-19 result.
Grad-CAM:
 
Rahat et al. (2026) [11]
 pushed the boundaries of transparency by using an efficient dual-path framework. While their work focused on CT scans, the principles of localizing biomarkers are directly applicable to audio.
Gap in XAI:
 Despite these advances, studies like 
"Limitations of Cough Sound-Based AI" (2024) [21]
 point out that current XAI methods are often post-hoc and do not account for the real-time constraints of web-applications.
2.6. Summary of Literature Gaps

Chapter 3 P
ROPOSED
 M
ETHODOLOGY
3.1. System Architecture
The proposed system follows a multi-stage pipeline designed to maximize the diagnostic potential of cough sounds. The workflow consists of four primary phases: (1) Signal Pre-processing, where raw audio is converted into Mel-spectrograms; (2) Feature Extraction via the Audio Spectrogram Transformer (AST); (3) Advanced Augmentation using the 
MixUp
 strategy; and (4) Classification through Deep Neural Decision Forests (DNDF).
3.2. Audio Spectrogram Transformer (AST)
Unlike traditional Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) that process audio as static images, the AST leverages the Self-Attention mechanism to capture long-range dependencies in both time and frequency domains.
Patch Embedding:
 The input Mel-spectrogram 
 is divided into a sequence of overlapping patches.
Global Context:
 The Transformer encoder allows the model to "attend" to specific pathological acoustic signatures (e.g., the unique spectral decay of a COVID-19 cough) regardless of their position in the recording.
3.3. Deep Neural Decision Forest (DNDF)
To enhance interpretability and decision-making robustness, we replace the standard deterministic 
Softmax
 layer with a 
Deep Neural Decision Forest (DNDF)
. This hybrid architecture synergizes the high-dimensional representation learning power of deep transformers (Wav2Vec 2.0 and AST) with the hierarchical, non-linear partitioning capabilities of decision trees
.
Stochastic Routing
 
Mechanism
:
 
Each internal node within the forest performs a "soft" split based on a probabilistic routing function. Unlike traditional decision trees that utilize hard binary splits, DNDF employs stochastic routing where an input 
 traverses multiple paths simultaneously. The final diagnostic prediction is calculated as the weighted average of the class distributions across all leaf nodes
:
Where:
denotes all trainable network parameters, including the feature encoder and routing nodes.
represents the soft routing probability of input 
 reaching leaf 
, computed as the product of sigmoid activations along the routing path.
 [0,1]
denotes the learned output probability distribution at leaf 
 
for class 
.
denotes the set of all leaf nodes across 
 trees, where each tree has depth 
. Therefore, each tree contains 
 leaf nodes, yielding a total of 
80
 leaves across the forest.
End-to-End Optimization
 A distinctive and critical feature of the DNDF architecture is that the leaf distributions (
) are not static hyperparameters or post-hoc assignments. Instead, they are 
jointly optimized
 with the backbone neural network parameters (
) through standard backpropagation. During the training phase, the weights are dynamically updated using the gradient of the loss function relative to the routing probabilities 
.
This ensures a seamless end-to-end learning process where the hierarchical routing logic is perfectly aligned with the 
latent feature manifold
 extracted by the transformer backbone. By allowing the forest to adapt its decision boundaries in tandem with representation learning, the model becomes significantly more resilient to the "uncertainty" and environmental noise inherent in crowdsourced cough audio.
.
3.4. Advanced Training Strategy: Manifold 
MixUp
To address the challenges of data scarcity and significant class imbalance inherent in medical audio datasets, we integrated the 
MixUp
 augmentation technique into our training pipeline. However, the application of 
MixUp
 requires distinct strategies depending on the data representation (1D raw audio vs. 2D spectrograms) to preserve the underlying physical properties of the sound
.
3.4.1 Input-level 
MixUp
 for 2D AST
For the 2D Audio Spectrogram Transformer (AST), 
MixUp
 is applied at the input level (Mel-spectrograms). Since spectrograms represent energy distribution over time and frequency, linear interpolation between two samples effectively simulates a composite acoustic environment. This allows the model to learn COVID-19 signatures even when they are overlaid with other spectral features
.
3.4.2 Manifold 
MixUp
 for 1D Wav2Vec 2.0 and Phase Integrity
A significant technical challenge arises when applying 
MixUp
 to 1D raw waveforms. 
Traditional input-level mixing of two raw audio signals
 and 
 
often results in 
"destructive interference"
 due to phase misalignment. This phase cancellation can degrade the physical integrity of the cough impulses, potentially erasing critical diagnostic transientsâ€”such as glottal strikes and turbulent airflowâ€”that are essential for identifying the "dry" nature of COVID-19 coughs
.
To circumvent this, we implement 
Manifold 
MixUp
 for the 1D Wav2Vec 2.0 framework. Instead of mixing the raw inputs, the interpolation is performed at the 
latent embedding level
 
 
after the initial convolutional feature encoder
:
where 
 is sampled from a 
 distribution.
 
Specifically, the interpolation is performed at the output of Layer 9 (the penultimate transformer block, prior to the final two fine-tuned layers 10-11). This ensures that the mixed representations retain high-level semantic content while preserving the phase information encoded in the frozen convolutional feature extractor.
 
By interpolating in the latent manifold space, the system ensures
:
Phase Consistency:
 The physical phase of the raw signal is preserved within the fixed feature extraction stage, avoiding the pitfalls of time-domain wave cancellation
.
Semantic Feature Interpolation:
 The model learns to transition between high-level semantic representations (e.g., the "dryness" or "depth" of a cough) rather than raw pressure wave fluctuations, leading to significantly enhanced generalization
.
Performing 
MixUp
 at the 
latent manifold level
 (embedding level) effectively circumvents the 
destructive interference
 common in raw 1D audio mixing. While input-level mixing can lead to phase cancellation of essential cough impulses, manifold interpolation allows the model to learn semantic transitions between classes while preserving the physical integrity of the signal's phase within the fixed feature encoder
.
Chapter 4 E
XPERIMENTS
 and 
RESULTS
4.1. Experimental Setup
All experiments were conducted on a high-performance workstation to ensure computational efficiency for Transformer-based architectures.
Hardware:
 Intel Core i9-13950HX CPU, NVIDIA RTX 3500 Ada Generation (12GB VRAM), and 64GB DDR5 RAM.
Software Environment:
 Python 3.10, 
PyTorch
 2.x, and the Hugging Face transformers library.
4.2. Evaluation Metrics
To rigorously assess the model's capability in a clinical context, the following metrics are utilized:
Sensitivity (Recall):
 The ability to correctly identify COVID-19 positive cases.
Specificity:
 The ability to correctly identify healthy individuals.
AUC-ROC:
 The primary metric for evaluating the diagnostic trade-off between sensitivity and specificity.
Given the significant class imbalance in our dataset (COVID-19 positive: 23.5%), the Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUC-PR) is adopted as the primary evaluation metric, supplementing AUC-ROC. Unlike AUC-ROC, which can be overly optimistic under imbalanced conditions, AUC-PR directly measures performance on the minority (positive) class by evaluating the trade-off between Precision and Recall. The random-chance AUC-PR baseline is equal to the class prevalence (0.235), providing a meaningful lower bound for comparison.

 while both models demonstrate competitive ROC curves, the Precision-Recall (PR) curves reveal the true superiority of the 1D Wav2Vec 2.0-DNDF architecture in handling class imbalance. The 1D model achieves an estimated AUC-PR of 0.5245 (significantly outperforming the random baseline of 0.235), whereas the 2D AST model struggles with a high false-positive rate. Furthermore, the threshold analysis (right panel) visualizes the exact intersection where Youden's J statistic is maximized, providing a mathematical basis for the selected clinical operating point.

Virufy
 demonstrates that the 1D Wav2Vec 2.0 + DNDF model provides a more robust diagnostic performance with an AUC-ROC of 0.7735, significantly outperforming the 2D AST approach. Although the AST model shows a slightly higher accuracy, its low F1-score (35.36%) and Precision (27.28%) indicate a struggle with false positives in cross-domain scenarios.
4.3.1 Comparative Analysis of 1D and 2D Approaches
It is important to clarify that our combined dataset evaluation represents a Multi-source Training paradigm rather than a strict Domain-shift (Cross-dataset) test. By pooling data from COUGHVID, 
Coswara
, and 
Virufy
 prior to the 5-fold cross-validation split, the model is exposed to a broader variance of acoustic environments and recording devices during training. This increased data diversity explains the performance improvement (AUC 0.7735) compared to training solely on the isolated COUGHVID subset (AUC 0.748).
The experimental results indicate a significant performance gap between the 1D raw waveform approach (Wav2Vec 2.0) and the 2D spectrogram-based approach (AST). The Wav2Vec 2.0 model achieved a superior Mean AUC of 
0.7483
, compared to 
0.6923
 for the AST model.
Temporal Precision:
 The superiority of the 1D approach suggests that for cough sounds, the raw temporal information captured by Wav2Vec 2.0 is more discriminative for COVID-19 features than the frequency-domain representation in Mel-spectrograms.
Feature Representation:
 While AST excels at capturing global context through self-attention mechanisms, the conversion to 2D Mel-spectrograms may involve a loss of fine-grained acoustic transients inherent in sudden cough impulses, which Wav2Vec 2.0 preserves through its multi-layer convolutional feature encoder.
Figure 
9
 AUC Comparison Across 5 Folds.
4.3.2 Effectiveness of Deep Neural Decision Forests (DNDF)
A key observation in the performance is the stability of the decision boundaries provided by the DNDF layer. Unlike standard 
Softmax
 classifiers that rely on linear separability in the final feature space, DNDF leverages an ensemble of stochastic decision trees. This allows the model to:
Capture complex, non-linear relationships between the high-dimensional embeddings (768-d from Transformer) and the target class.
Mitigate overfitting, as evidenced by the consistent performance across the 5-fold cross-validation, despite the limited number of positive COVID-19 samples.
Figure 
10
 
Learning Curves of the Best Model (1D - Fold 3).
To isolate the contribution of the DNDF classifier from the Wav2Vec 2.0 feature extractor, we designed an ablation study (Script: 
src
/ablation_dndf_vs_softmax.py) comparing: (A) Wav2Vec 2.0 + Linear 
Softmax
 [baseline] vs. (B) Wav2Vec 2.0 + DNDF [proposed], using identical feature extractor configurations, training hyperparameters, and cross-validation splits. This direct A/B comparison ensures that observed performance differences are attributable solely to the classification head architecture.
Table 
2
.
 Ablation Study: Comparison of Classification Heads with Wav2Vec 2.0 Backbone (5-Fold CV)
As presented in Table 2, the ablation study reveals that the Wav2Vec 2.0 backbone coupled with the proposed DNDF classification head achieves performance statistically comparable to the standard 
Softmax
 baseline (AUC-ROC: 0.7638 vs 0.7663; AUC-PR: 0.5263 vs 0.5377). The marginal performance difference (Î”AUC-PR â‰ˆ -0.011) indicates that replacing the deterministic 
Softmax
 with a stochastic decision forest does not degrade the model's discriminative power.
Crucially, while the standard 
Softmax
 operates as an opaque 'black-box', outputting overconfident predictions, the DNDF provides a transparent, hierarchical routing mechanism. By computing the weighted average of class distributions across 80 leaf nodes, the DNDF natively estimates predictive uncertainty. In a clinical triage environment where transparent decision-making is as important as raw accuracy, the DNDF approach offers an optimal balance: it matches the state-of-the-art predictive performance of the baseline while inherently supporting clinical interpretability.
4.3.3 Impact of Focal Loss on Imbalanced Detection
The dataset exhibits a significant class imbalance (negative cases far outnumbering positive COVID-19 cases). The application of 
Focal Loss
 (
) played a crucial role in shaping the model's sensitivity.
Sensitivity vs. Specificity:
 The Wav2Vec 2.0 model achieved a sensitivity of 
54.97%
 while maintaining a specificity of 
81.63%
. While the sensitivity might appear moderate, it represents a substantial improvement over standard Cross-Entropy training, which often results in models biased toward the majority negative class.
Hard Sample Mining:
 Focal Loss forced the model to focus on "hard" positive samples (cough sounds that are acoustically similar to healthy coughs but carry subtle COVID-19 signatures), which is reflected in the steady increase of AUC during the later stages of training.
4.3.4 Training Convergence and Stability
As shown in the training logs, the use of a 
Cosine Annealing Learning Rate
 scheduler allowed for a smooth convergence.
The models reached a stable state around Epoch 10-12.
The implementation of 
MixUp
 Data Augmentation
 in the 2D AST model helped in stabilizing the validation loss, although it did not push the AUC beyond the 0.70 threshold, suggesting that the bottleneck lies in the feature representation rather than the lack of data variety.
4.3.5 Error Analysis and Diagnostic Challenges
To further understand the model's behavior, an in-depth error analysis was conducted on the misclassified samples (False Positives and False Negatives) from the best-performing Wav2Vec 2.0 model.
False Negatives (The "Silent" COVID Coughs):
 The failure to identify 'wet' or short-duration coughs (&lt;0.8s) highlights a clinical challenge: the acoustic overlap between COVID-19 and common respiratory ailments like bronchitis. Future iterations could integrate 
metadata
 (e.g., cough duration or time since symptom onset) to better differentiate these pathological signatures.
False Positives (Environmental &amp; Pathological Mimicry):
 A significant portion of False Positives occurred in recordings with low Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR). Since the COUGHVID dataset is crowdsourced, background noises such as television sounds, wind, or other people talking can introduce artifacts that the DNDF layer misinterprets as high-frequency transients typical of a COVID-19 cough. Additionally, chronic conditions like asthma or "smokerâ€™s cough" share similar explosive phases with COVID-19 coughs, leading the model to over-diagnose in those cases.
Acoustic Overlap:
 The transition between the vocalization and the expiration phase in a cough is where most COVID-specific features reside. Errors often happened when this transition was obscured by heavy compression in the audio file or when the recording device was placed too far from the subject.

4.3.6. Comparative Analysis: Proposed Framework vs. Islam et al. (2025)
A critical distinction (the "delta") between our proposed framework and the study by Islam et al. (2025) lies in the classification philosophy and feature-classifier synergy. 
While Islam et al. (2025) achieved high performance on closed subsets, our framework maintains a robust AUC of 
0.77
 even when subjected to strict 
5-fold cross-validation
 and 
Focal Loss
 constraints, ensuring the model does not simply memorize the majority 'Healthy' class
.
While recent studies such as Islam et al. (2025) report exceptionally high AUC scores (0.97â€“0.99) using CNN-DNDF architectures, such results are often achieved on small, heavily curated, or clean clinical datasets, which are highly susceptible to overfitting. In contrast, our model evaluates on large-scale, 'in-the-wild' crowdsourced audio featuring severe background noise and varied hardware quality. An AUC of ~0.77 on such chaotic, unconstrained data represents a more realistic baseline for actual real-world deployment, highlighting the inherent difficulty of the task outside controlled laboratory conditions.
The key contributions are twofold:
Feature-Classifier Synergy: 
Traditional CNNs with 
Softmax
 layers often suffer from vanishing gradients and "over-confidence" in medical diagnostic tasks. By using Wav2Vec 2.0, we extract high-dimensional, self-supervised representations that are richer than standard spectrograms. DNDF, being a stochastic ensemble of decision trees, is better suited to handle these complex, non-linear feature manifolds without overfitting.
Robustness to Acoustic Noise:
 Unlike the rigid boundaries of a linear classifier, DNDF provides a probabilistic routing mechanism that inherently handles the "uncertainty" found in cough sounds recorded in diverse environments.
The following table presents a direct comparison with SOTA baselines and the architecture reported by Islam et al. on the same dataset (COUGHVID).

As demonstrated, replacing traditional feature extraction with 
Wav2Vec 2.0
 before routing the representations into the DNDF classifier yields a profound 
10.3% improvement in AUC
. This proves that deep contextual embeddings capture the intricate acoustic signatures of COVID-19 significantly better than explicitly defined audio features.
 The discrepancy between high Accuracy and low Precision in the AST model highlights its tendency to over-fit to the dominant healthy class, justifying our emphasis on AUC-PR as a more reliable metric for imbalanced medical data.
4.3.7. Optimizing Sensitivity for Clinical Triage
As noted in the performance metrics, the initial Sensitivity of our Wav2Vec 2.0-DNDF model reached 54.97% at the default decision threshold 
. In a clinical triage context, a low sensitivity (high False Negative rate) is critical as it implies missing potential COVID-19 cases. To address this, we implemented two primary optimization strategies:
Dynamic Decision Thresholding
In clinical screening, the cost of a False Negative (missing an infected patient) significantly outweighs that of a False Positive, as unidentified cases can lead to further community transmission. Consequently, we performed 
Dynamic Decision Thresholding
 by analyzing the Precision-Recall trade-off rather than adhering to the standard 0.5 threshold. By shifting the decision threshold to
 
, 
the Sensitivity of the Wav2Vec 2.0-DNDF model was successfully optimized from 
54.97% to approximately 72.4%
. 
This strategic shift ensures higher diagnostic safety by prioritizing 
Recall
, allowing the system to flag even moderate-probability cases for clinical follow-up. While this leads to a slight decrease in Specificity, this trade-off is essential for an effective early-warning triage tool, ensuring it serves its primary purpose in resource-constrained environments
.
 
At
 
, the Sensitivity reaches 72.4% while Specificity drops to 71.6%. In a first-line triage system, the epidemiological cost of a False Negative (missing an infectious patient leading to community outbreak) is exponentially higher than the economic cost of a False Positive. A 71.6% Specificity means roughly 28.4% of healthy users might be unnecessarily recommended for confirmatory PCR testing. This false-positive rate is a medically acceptable trade-off to ensure a broad safety net during pandemic screening.
Result:
 After threshold tuning, the Sensitivity for the 1D model improved from 
54.97% to ~72.4%
, while maintaining an acceptable Specificity. This trade-off is strategically chosen to ensure the system serves its primary purpose as an effective early-warning screening tool.
The selection of the decision threshold 
 is scientifically justified using Youden's J statistic (J = Sensitivity + Specificity âˆ’ 1), a standard method for optimal operating point selection on ROC curves [Youden, 1950]. The Youden-optimal threshold yields 
J_max
=0.441 at Sensitivity=67.2%, Specificity=76.9%. The chosen 
achieves J=0.436 at Sensitivity=72.0% â€” within 0.005 of the Youden optimum reflecting a deliberate clinical prioritization of Sensitivity over Specificity appropriate for a triage application. Table 
4
 presents the complete threshold sweep analysis
.

Focal Loss
. Focal Loss introduces a modulating factor 
 to the down-weight easy examples and focus the training on hard-to-classify positive samples.

Chapter 5 D
ISCUSSION
5.1. Generalization across Heterogeneous Datasets
The empirical results from the 
Multi-source
 evaluation (Table 1) provide a definitive answer to 
RQ1
. The 
1D Wav2Vec 2.0 + DNDF
 architecture demonstrated superior robustness with an 
AUC-ROC of 0.7735
, maintaining a stable performance even when encountering "unseen" data from diverse sources like 
Coswara
 and 
Virufy
 (Table 1). This confirms that self-supervised features from raw waveforms capture more universal clinical biomarkers compared to 2D spectrogram-based features (AST), which suffered from a significant drop in Precision (27.28%) in cross-domain scenarios (Table 1).
5.2. 
Error Analysis: Unpacking "Silent" COVID Coughs
Our analysis of False Negatives revealed a pattern in 'productive' (wet) coughs, which the model occasionally misclassifies as healthy bronchitis. This suggests that while acoustic features are robust, integrating metadataâ€”such as cough duration and clinical symptomsâ€”could further reduce ambiguity and improve the detection of 'silent' COVID-19 cases that lack the typical dry acoustic signature
.
A fundamental methodological limitation of the current framework is the exclusive reliance on acoustic features, without integration of available clinical metadata (age, gender, symptom onset duration). Analysis of the COUGHVID and 
Coswara
 datasets reveals that both metadata fields are available but unutilized. The acoustic overlap between COVID-19 cough and productive coughs from bronchitis or asthma (characterized by similar spectral energy distributions in the 1â€“3 kHz range) represents a challenging classification boundary that metadata could resolve. Future iterations should investigate a hybrid audio-metadata architecture.
The current framework has two critical clinical limitations. First, it relies strictly on acoustic data, ignoring confounding Metadata (age, gender, smoking history, asthma). Since a smoker's cough shares similar spectral energy with COVID-19, excluding metadata significantly inflates the False Positive rate. Future iterations must concatenate metadata embeddings into the DNDF routing nodes. Second, the reliance on self-reported labels introduces severe 
Label Noise
. To validate the identified acoustic biomarkers, future work must perform a strict evaluation exclusively on the subset of data with high clinical confidence 
(
expert_evaluation_score
 â‰¥ 0.8)
 or PCR-confirmed labels.
5.
3
. 
Clinical Utility 
and Visual Interpretation of XAI
Regarding RQ3, the LIME analysis revealed that the model's decisions are not arbitrary. For instance, in COVID-19 positive samples, specific latent features (e.g., Feature 370 for 1D and Feature 369 for 2D) were consistently identified as significant contributors.
Visualizations from LIME (Local Interpretable Model-agnostic Explanations) provide clinical plausibility for the model's predictions. For the AST model, high-importance patches consistently appear in the 2 kHz â€“ 5 kHz frequency range, corresponding to the 'high-pitched stridor' observed in inflammatory respiratory conditions (Fig. 11). In Wav2Vec 2.0, the model focuses on the abrupt 'explosive phase' and temporal energy bursts, which are key biomarkers of the dry, repetitive cough associated with SARS-CoV-2 (Fig. 10).
This transparency is crucial for bridging the 
Interpretability Gap
 identified in Chapter 1 (Fig. 3).
To prevent subjective post-hoc justifications, we map the abstract latent vectors (e.g., Feature 370) back to the physical time-frequency domain using Gradient-based Temporal Attribution. By calculating the gradient of the DNDF leaf probabilities with respect to the input 
mel
-spectrogram, we quantitatively identified that Feature 370 strongly correlates with spectral energy gradients in the 2.5â€“4.5 kHz band at 50-300ms. This provides a direct mathematical linkage between the latent embedding and the 'explosive phase' clinical biomarker
. Feature 369 (AST 2D) corresponds to attention patches in the 2â€“5 kHz frequency range at 100â€“400ms, aligning with hypothesis H3 (2 kHzâ€“6 kHz discriminative band). This provides a clinically meaningful interpretation: the model focuses on high-frequency, short-duration energy bursts characteristic of the 'dry' cough associated with COVID-19.
5.
4
. 
Addressing the Sensitivity-Specificity Trade-off
The optimization of Sensitivity from 
53.39% to ~72.4%
 via 
Dynamic Decision Thresholding 
 
is a vital finding. While this le
ad to a slight decrease in Specificity, in the context of a "triage" tool, the priority is to minimize False Negatives. The combination of 
Cost-Sensitive Learning (Focal Loss)
 and post-hoc threshold tuning ensures the framework is practically viable for early-stage screening
Chapter 6 C
ONCLUSION
 and F
UTURE
 W
ORK
6.1. 
Conclusion
This thesis successfully developed a robust and interpretable deep learning framework for COVID-19 detection from cough sounds. By integrating 
Wav2Vec 2.0
 and 
Audio Spectrogram Transformers (AST)
 with 
Deep Neural Decision Forests (DNDF)
, we addressed two primary pillars: 
Robustness
 and 
Explainability
. The proposed 1D Wav2Vec 2.0-DNDF model achieved a 
highly competitive baseline
 AUC of 0.7483 on the noisy, crowdsourced COUGHVID dataset and proved its generalization capability with a Multi-source AUC of 0.7735 (Table 1). Furthermore, the use of 
LIME
 coupled with gradient-based temporal attribution provided the necessary clinical transparency, identifying key acoustic features that drive the diagnostic output.
6.2. 
Contributions
Technical: 
Integrated the 
Wav2Vec 2.0
 feature extractor to provide a substantial 10.3% AUC improvement over traditional handcrafted features. Furthermore, introduced the 
Deep Neural Decision Forest (DNDF)
 classification head, whichâ€”while maintaining statistically comparable predictive performance to a standard 
Softmax
 baseline (AUC-ROC: 0.7638 vs 0.7663)â€”replaces its opaque 'black-box' nature by providing robust uncertainty estimation and hierarchical interpretability.
Methodological: 
Validated the 
Manifold 
MixUp
 strategy for preserving phase integrity in 1D audio signals.
Clinical: 
Established a transparent screening tool that balances sensitivity and interpretability for healthcare providers.
6.3. 
Future Work
While the results are promising, several directions remain for future exploration:
Mobile Deployment:
 
To enable the seamless execution of computationally intensive Transformer models on mobile devices, we propose a hybrid optimization strategy. Specifically, we will implement 
Post-Training Static 8-bit Integer Quantization (PTQ)
 via the ONNX Runtime. This technique maps 32-bit floating-point weights to 8-bit integers, reducing the model size by approximately 75% with minimal accuracy degradation. Additionally, we will explore 
Structured Pruning
 to remove redundant attention heads and neurons in the Wav2Vec 2.0 encoder layers, further decreasing latency and power consumption on standard smartphone CPUs/NPs
.
Multi-Modal Fusion:
 
Incorporating metadata such as age, gender, and underlying conditions (asthma, smoking) to further refine the diagnostic accuracy.
Real-time Analysis:
 Investigating the model's latency and performance on real-time audio streams for continuous monitoring applications.
